					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Scott Morrison</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 77 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-77" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">77 languages</span>
	</label>
	<div class="vector-dropdown-content">

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Scott_Morrison_sidebar" title="Template:Scott Morrison sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Scott_Morrison_sidebar" title="Template talk:Scott Morrison sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Scott_Morrison_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Scott John Morrison</b> (born 13 May 1968) is an Australian politician who served as the 30th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a> from 2018 to 2022, holding office as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Leader of the Liberal Party of Australia">leader of the Liberal Party of Australia</a>. He is currently the <a href="/wiki/Member_of_parliament" title="Member of parliament">member of parliament</a> (MP) for the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a> seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Cook" title="Division of Cook">Cook</a>, a position he has held since 2007.
</p><p>Morrison was born in <a href="/wiki/Sydney" title="Sydney">Sydney</a> and studied <a href="/wiki/Economic_geography" title="Economic geography">economic geography</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales" title="University of New South Wales">University of New South Wales</a>. He worked as director of the New Zealand Office of Tourism and Sport from 1998 to 2000 and was managing director of <a href="/wiki/Tourism_Australia" title="Tourism Australia">Tourism Australia</a> from 2004 to 2006. Morrison also was state director of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_(New_South_Wales_Division)" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia (New South Wales Division)">New South Wales Liberal Party</a> from 2000 to 2004. He was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 election</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_parliament" title="Member of parliament">member of parliament</a> (MP) for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Cook" title="Division of Cook">division of Cook</a> in New South Wales, and was quickly appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Shadow_cabinet_of_Australia" title="Shadow cabinet of Australia">shadow cabinet</a>.
</p><p>After the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-National coalition</a>'s victory at the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 election</a>, Morrison was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Immigration,_Citizenship,_Migrant_Services_and_Multicultural_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Immigration, Citizenship, Migrant Services and Multicultural Affairs">Minister for Immigration and Border Protection</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government" title="Abbott government">Abbott government</a>, where he was responsible for implementing <a href="/wiki/Operation_Sovereign_Borders" title="Operation Sovereign Borders">Operation Sovereign Borders</a>. In a reshuffle the following year, he became <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Social_Services_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Social Services (Australia)">Minister for Social Services</a> where he oversaw the introduction of the unlawful <a href="/wiki/Robodebt_scheme" title="Robodebt scheme">Robodebt scheme</a>. He was later promoted to the role of <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a> in September 2015, after <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a> replaced Abbott as prime minister. In August 2018, Home Affairs Minister <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> unsuccessfully challenged Turnbull for the leadership of the Liberal Party. Leadership tensions continued, and the party voted to hold <a href="/wiki/2018_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spills" title="2018 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills">a second leadership ballot</a> days later, with Turnbull choosing not to stand. In that ballot, Morrison was seen as a compromise candidate and defeated both Dutton and Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Julie_Bishop" title="Julie Bishop">Julie Bishop</a> to become party leader and thus prime minister in August 2018.
</p><p>Morrison won a second term after leading the Coalition to an <a href="/wiki/Upset_victory" class="mw-redirect" title="Upset victory">upset victory</a> in the <a href="/wiki/2019_Australian_federal_election" title="2019 Australian federal election">2019 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Morrison drew near unanimous condemnation for taking a holiday during <a href="/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Australian_bushfire_season" title="2019–20 Australian bushfire season">Australia's 2019–20 bushfire season</a> and for his government's response to the disaster.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Morrison also faced criticism for his response to the <a href="/wiki/2021_Australian_Parliament_House_sexual_misconduct_allegations" title="2021 Australian Parliament House sexual misconduct allegations">2021 Parliament sexual misconduct allegations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Australia">COVID-19 pandemic</a>, Morrison established the <a href="/wiki/National_Cabinet_(Australia)" title="National Cabinet (Australia)">National Cabinet</a>, and Australia received praise during 2020 for being one of the few Western countries to successfully suppress the virus,<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> though the slow initial pace of the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_Australia" title="COVID-19 vaccination in Australia">COVID-19 vaccination rollout</a> was criticised.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> In foreign policy, Morrison oversaw the signing of the <a href="/wiki/AUKUS" title="AUKUS">AUKUS</a> security pact and increased tensions between <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93China_relations" title="Australia–China relations">Australia and China</a><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> and Australia and France.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Morrison directed logistical support to Ukraine as part of the international effort against Russia in the wake of the <a href="/wiki/2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine" class="mw-redirect" title="2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine">2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine</a>. Morrison has also been criticised for his government's response to the <a href="/wiki/2022_eastern_Australia_floods" title="2022 eastern Australia floods">2022 eastern Australia floods</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and his perceived inaction on <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Australia" title="Climate change in Australia">climate change</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The government was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election" title="2022 Australian federal election">2022 election</a> and Morrison stepped down as leader of the Liberal Party; Peter Dutton was <a href="/wiki/2022_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="2022 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">elected unopposed</a> to replace him.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After leaving office, Morrison became involved in a scandal after it was revealed that <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison_ministerial_positions_controversy" title="Scott Morrison ministerial positions controversy">he had secretly held several ministerial positions while serving as prime minister</a>, which led to Parliament passing a <a href="/wiki/Censure" title="Censure">censure</a> motion against him.<sup id="cite_ref-Karp_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Karp-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<p>Morrison was born in the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Waverley,_New_South_Wales" title="Waverley, New South Wales">Waverley</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sydney" title="Sydney">Sydney</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> the younger of two sons born to Marion (née Smith) and <a href="/wiki/John_Douglas_Morrison" title="John Douglas Morrison">John Douglas Morrison</a> (1934–2020).<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> His father was a policeman who served on the <a href="/wiki/Waverley_Municipal_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Waverley Municipal Council">Waverley Municipal Council</a>, including a single term as mayor.<sup id="cite_ref-bryant_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bryant-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Morrison's maternal grandfather was born in New Zealand.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> His paternal grandmother was the niece of noted Australian poet Dame <a href="/wiki/Mary_Gilmore" title="Mary Gilmore">Mary Gilmore</a>. In 2012, on the 50th anniversary of her death, he delivered a tribute to her in federal parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Morrison is descended from William Roberts, a <a href="/wiki/Convicts_in_Australia" title="Convicts in Australia">convict</a> who was convicted of stealing yarn and <a href="/wiki/Penal_transportation" title="Penal transportation">transported</a> to Australia on the <a href="/wiki/First_Fleet" title="First Fleet">First Fleet</a> in 1788.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morrison grew up in the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Bronte,_New_South_Wales" title="Bronte, New South Wales">Bronte</a>. He had a brief career as a child actor, appearing in several television commercials and small roles in local shows.<sup id="cite_ref-rise_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rise-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Morrison attended <a href="/wiki/Sydney_Boys_High_School" title="Sydney Boys High School">Sydney Boys High School</a> before going on to complete a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Science" title="Bachelor of Science">Bachelor of Science</a> (B.Sc.) <a href="/wiki/Honours_degree#Australia" title="Honours degree">honours degree</a> in applied <a href="/wiki/Economic_geography" title="Economic geography">economic geography</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales" title="University of New South Wales">University of New South Wales</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Morrison_Au_parl_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morrison_Au_parl-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> His honours thesis, a demographical analysis of <a href="/wiki/Plymouth_Brethren" title="Plymouth Brethren">Christian Brethren</a> assemblies in Sydney, was deposited in the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Manchester_Library" title="University of Manchester Library">University of Manchester Library</a>'s Christian Brethren Collection.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Morrison contemplated studying theology at <a href="/wiki/Regent_College" title="Regent College">Regent College</a> in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>, Canada, but he instead chose to enter the workforce after completing his undergraduate education, in part due to the disapproval of his father.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span></h2>
<p>After graduating from university, Morrison worked as national policy and research manager for the <a href="/wiki/Property_Council_of_Australia" title="Property Council of Australia">Property Council of Australia</a> from 1989 to 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-Morrison_Au_parl_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morrison_Au_parl-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He then moved into tourism, serving as deputy chief executive of the Australian Tourism Task Force and then general manager of the Tourism Council of Australia; the latter was managed by <a href="/wiki/Bruce_Baird" title="Bruce Baird">Bruce Baird</a>, whom he would eventually succeed in federal parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-rise_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rise-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1998, Morrison moved to New Zealand to become director of the newly created Office of Tourism and Sport. He formed a close relationship with New Zealand's <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Tourism_(New_Zealand)" title="Minister of Tourism (New Zealand)">tourism minister</a>, <a href="/wiki/Murray_McCully" title="Murray McCully">Murray McCully</a>, and was involved with the creation of the long-running "<a href="/wiki/Tourism_New_Zealand#100%_Pure_New_Zealand" title="Tourism New Zealand">100% Pure New Zealand</a>" campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-rise_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rise-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-rnz2018_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rnz2018-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He left this position in 2000, a year before the contract schedule.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morrison returned to Australia in 2000, to become state director of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_(New_South_Wales_Division)" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia (New South Wales Division)">New South Wales division</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party</a>. He oversaw the party's campaigns in the <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001 federal election</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/2003_New_South_Wales_state_election" title="2003 New South Wales state election">2003 New South Wales state election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bryant_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bryant-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tourism_Australia">Tourism Australia</span></h3>
<p>In 2004, Morrison left the NSW Liberal Party post to become the inaugural managing director of <a href="/wiki/Tourism_Australia" title="Tourism Australia">Tourism Australia</a>, which had been established by the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a>. His appointment was controversial due to its openly political nature.<sup id="cite_ref-bryant_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bryant-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He signed an initial three-year contract.<sup id="cite_ref-middleton_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-middleton-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Morrison approved and defended the contentious "<a href="/wiki/So_where_the_bloody_hell_are_you%3F" title="So where the bloody hell are you?">So where the bloody hell are you?</a>" advertising campaign featuring <a href="/wiki/Lara_Bingle" class="mw-redirect" title="Lara Bingle">Lara Bingle</a>. His contract was terminated in July 2006, which at the time was attributed to conflict with tourism minister <a href="/wiki/Fran_Bailey" title="Fran Bailey">Fran Bailey</a> over the government's plans to further integrate the agency into the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Public_Service" title="Australian Public Service">Australian Public Service</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> He had been awarded a pay rise by the Remuneration Tribunal three weeks before his sacking. A 2019 investigation by <i><a href="/wiki/The_Saturday_Paper" title="The Saturday Paper">The Saturday Paper</a></i> suggested Morrison was sacked due to concerns that Tourism Australia was not following government procurement guidelines for three contracts relating to the "So where the bloody hell are you?" campaign, with a total value of $184 million. A 2008 report from the <a href="/wiki/Auditor-General_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Auditor-General of Australia">Auditor-General</a> found that "information had been kept from the board, procurement guidelines breached and private companies engaged before paperwork was signed and without appropriate value-for-money assessments". It was suggested that <a href="/wiki/M%26C_Saatchi" title="M&amp;C Saatchi">M&amp;C Saatchi</a>, which had previously worked with Morrison on the "100% Pure" campaign in New Zealand, received favourable treatment in the tendering process.<sup id="cite_ref-middleton_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-middleton-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, following the <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison_ministerial_positions_controversy" title="Scott Morrison ministerial positions controversy">ministerial positions controversy</a>, Fran Bailey revealed that Morrison showed no respect for his colleagues at Tourism Australia and he left her feeling bullied, also confirming that he “point-blank refused” to provide her or the board with any documentation or to answer questions about how the ad campaign was awarded to Saatchi.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This episode and, more generally, his career in marketing led to his satirical <a href="/wiki/Sobriquet" title="Sobriquet">sobriquet</a>, "Scotty from Marketing,"<sup id="cite_ref-smith_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
originating with the <a href="/wiki/News_satire" title="News satire">satirical news</a> website <i><a href="/wiki/The_Betoota_Advocate" title="The Betoota Advocate">The Betoota Advocate</a></i> in August 2018. It was taken up on <a href="/wiki/Twitter" title="Twitter">Twitter</a> in early 2019, and spiked at the height of the bushfire crisis on 29 December 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In January 2020, Morrison referred to the name as a "snarky comment" used by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor Party</a> to discredit him.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-smith_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smith-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_career">Political career</span></h2>
<h3><span id="Opposition_.282007.E2.80.932013.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_(2007–2013)">Opposition (2007–2013)</span></h3>
<p>Morrison sought Liberal <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselection</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Cook" title="Division of Cook">division of Cook</a>, an electorate in the southern suburbs of Sydney which includes <a href="/wiki/Cronulla,_New_South_Wales" title="Cronulla, New South Wales">Cronulla</a>, <a href="/wiki/Caringbah" title="Caringbah">Caringbah</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Miranda,_New_South_Wales" title="Miranda, New South Wales">Miranda</a>, for the 2007 election, following the retirement of <a href="/wiki/Bruce_Baird" title="Bruce Baird">Bruce Baird</a>, who had been the member since 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> He lost the ballot to Michael Towke, a telecommunications engineer and the candidate of the Liberals' right faction, by 82 votes to 8.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Paul_Fletcher_(politician)" title="Paul Fletcher (politician)">Paul Fletcher</a> who came closest to Towke received 70 votes. Fletcher went on to win Liberal preselection for the North Shore seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Bradfield" title="Division of Bradfield">Bradfield</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, allegations surfaced that Towke had engaged in <a href="/wiki/Branch_stacking" title="Branch stacking">branch stacking</a> and had embellished his resume.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The state executive of the Liberal Party disendorsed Towke and held a new pre-selection ballot, which Morrison won. The allegations subsequently proved to be false, and <i><a href="/wiki/The_Daily_Telegraph_(Sydney)" title="The Daily Telegraph (Sydney)">The Daily Telegraph</a></i> was forced to pay an undisclosed amount to settle a defamation suit filed by Towke.<sup id="cite_ref-SMH_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SMH-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> At the general election, Morrison suffered a two-party swing of over six percent against <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> candidate <a href="/wiki/Mark_Buttigieg" title="Mark Buttigieg">Mark Buttigieg</a>, but was able to retain the seat on the strength of winning 52 percent of the primary vote.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In 2022, Towke accused Morrison of engaging in "racial vilification" during the 2007 preselection, including "saying Mr Morrison told party members they should not vote for him because he was from a Lebanese family and because of rumours he was a Muslim", a claim Morrison denied.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Scott_Morrison.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Scott_Morrison.jpg/170px-Scott_Morrison.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="158" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Scott_Morrison.jpg/255px-Scott_Morrison.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Scott_Morrison.jpg/340px-Scott_Morrison.jpg 2x" data-file-width="342" data-file-height="317" /></a><figcaption>Morrison in 2009</figcaption></figure>
<p>In September 2008, Morrison was appointed to <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">coalition</a> front bench as shadow minister for housing and local government.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In December 2009, he became shadow minister for immigration and citizenship, coming into the shadow cabinet for the first time during <a href="/wiki/Tony_Abbott" title="Tony Abbott">Tony Abbott</a>'s first cabinet reshuffle shortly after winning the leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2010, forty-eight <a href="/wiki/Asylum_seeker" title="Asylum seeker">asylum seekers</a> died in the <a href="/wiki/2010_Christmas_Island_boat_disaster" title="2010 Christmas Island boat disaster">Christmas Island boat disaster</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> In February 2011, Morrison publicly questioned the decision of the <a href="/wiki/Gillard_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Gillard Government">Gillard Labor government</a> to pay for the relatives of the victims to travel to funerals in Sydney, arguing that the same privilege was not extended to Australian citizens. After fellow Liberal and shadow treasurer <a href="/wiki/Joe_Hockey" title="Joe Hockey">Joe Hockey</a> disagreed with Morrison's statements, Morrison said that the timing of his comments was insensitive, but did not back away from the comments themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Other Liberal Party members including former prime minister <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> and former opposition leader <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a> also criticised Morrison for his comments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly202115_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly202115-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In the same month, it was revealed that Morrison had "urged the shadow cabinet to capitalise on the electorate's growing concerns" about Muslims and appeal to the public perception of their "inability to integrate" to gain votes.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2013, Morrison said that the police should be notified of where asylum seekers are living in the community if any antisocial behaviour has occurred, and that there should be strict guidelines for the behaviour of those currently on bridging visas while they await the determination of their claims.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The new code of conduct was released by the immigration minister for more than 20,000 irregular maritime arrivals living in the community on bridging visas.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (August 2018)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Abbott_government_.282013.E2.80.932015.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Abbott_government_(2013–2015)">Abbott government (2013–2015)</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg/220px-Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="121" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg/330px-Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg/440px-Scott_Morrison_swearing_in_2014.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="352" /></a><figcaption>Morrison is sworn in as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Social_Services_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Social Services (Australia)">Minister for Social Services</a> by <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> <a href="/wiki/Peter_Cosgrove" title="Peter Cosgrove">Sir Peter Cosgrove</a>, 2014</figcaption></figure>
<p>Following the Coalition's victory at the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 federal election</a>, Morrison was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Immigration_and_Border_Protection" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Immigration and Border Protection">Minister for Immigration and Border Protection</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government" title="Abbott government">Abbott government</a> and included in <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">cabinet</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Morrison_Au_parl_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morrison_Au_parl-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Based on a series of off-the-record interviews, in June 2014 Morrison was identified by <a href="/wiki/Fairfax_Media" title="Fairfax Media">Fairfax Media</a> as the leader of an informal grouping of "economically moderate, or <a href="/wiki/Wets_and_dries" title="Wets and dries">wet</a>" government MPs, also including <a href="/wiki/Greg_Hunt" title="Greg Hunt">Greg Hunt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stuart_Robert" title="Stuart Robert">Stuart Robert</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Josh_Frydenberg" title="Josh Frydenberg">Josh Frydenberg</a>. It was linked with another moderate grouping led by <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Pyne" title="Christopher Pyne">Christopher Pyne</a>. It was further reported that Morrison had unsuccessfully argued in cabinet for a $25 million bailout of <a href="/wiki/SPC_Ardmona" class="mw-redirect" title="SPC Ardmona">SPC Ardmona</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span></h4>
<p>On 18 September 2013, Morrison launched <a href="/wiki/Operation_Sovereign_Borders" title="Operation Sovereign Borders">Operation Sovereign Borders</a>, the new government's strategy aimed at stopping unauthorised boats from entering Australian waters.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Cabinet documents from this time revealed in 2018 that Morrison asked for mitigation strategies to avoid granting permanent visas to 700 refugees.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> His office reported that there were 300 boats and 20,587 arrivals in 2013 to only 1 boat and 157 arrivals for all of 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Refugees" title="United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees">UNHCR</a> expressed concerns that the practice may violate the <a href="/wiki/Convention_Relating_to_the_Status_of_Refugees" title="Convention Relating to the Status of Refugees">Refugee Convention</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>  In September 2014, it was reported that zero asylum seekers had died at sea since December 2013, compared with more than 1,100 deaths between 2008 and 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The annual refugee intake, which had been increased to 20,000 for 2012–13 by the previous government, was reduced to 13,750, the level it had been in 2011–12. Morrison stated that "Not one of those places will go to anyone who comes on a boat to Australia [...] they will go to people who have come the right way."<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morrison defended his use of the terms "illegal arrivals" and "illegal boats," saying that "I've always referred to illegal entry ... I've never claimed that it's illegal to claim asylum."<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his time as Immigration Minister, Morrison's dealings with the media and accountability to the public were widely criticised by journalists, Labor and Greens senators, and others for refusing to provide details about the matters within his portfolio. Morrison asserted that to reveal details of operations would be to play into the hands of people smugglers who used this information to plan illegal smuggling operations.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> On many occasions Morrison refused to answer questions about the status of asylum seekers or boats coming to and from Australia, often on the basis that he would not disclose "on water" or "operational" matters.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2014, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Human_Rights_Commission" title="Australian Human Rights Commission">Australian Human Rights Commission</a> delivered a report to the government which found that Morrison failed in his responsibility to act in the best interests of children in detention during his time as Minister. The overarching finding of the inquiry was that the prolonged, mandatory detention of asylum seeker children caused them significant mental and physical illness and developmental delays, in breach of Australia's international obligations.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> The report was criticised by Tony Abbott as being politically motivated, with regard to the timing of the report's release after the Abbott government had taken office. The government released the report publicly in February 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early December 2014, Morrison had the <i>Migration and Maritime Powers Legislation Amendment (Resolving the Asylum Legacy Caseload) Bill 2014</i> passed through the Australian Parliament. The bill gave Morrison more power than any previous minister in dealing with people seeking asylum in Australia, including the power to return asylum seekers to their place of origin, detain asylum seekers without charge, and refuse asylum seekers who arrive by boat access to the Refugee Review Tribunal.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> The bill reintroduced <a href="/wiki/Temporary_protection_visa" title="Temporary protection visa">temporary protection visas</a> to deal specifically with the backlog of 30,000 people who had arrived under the previous Labor government but who had yet to be processed. The bill allowed those on bridging visas to apply for work, and increased the refugee intake to 18,750.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_services">Social services</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg/170px-Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg/255px-Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg/340px-Scott_Morrison_2014.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3378" data-file-height="4123" /></a><figcaption>Morrison in 2014</figcaption></figure>
<p>In a cabinet reshuffle in late December 2014, Morrison was appointed the <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Social_Services_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Social Services (Australia)">Minister for Social Services</a> and ceased to be Minister for Immigration and Border Protection.<sup id="cite_ref-smabc_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smabc-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> As minister, Morrison changed indexation changes to the Family Tax Benefits payment.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> In April 2015, he announced the introduction of the "<a href="/wiki/No_Jab,_No_Pay" title="No Jab, No Pay">No Jab, No Pay</a>" policy, which withholds family and childcare benefits from parents who do not vaccinate their children.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> His time as minister was criticised by his opposition counterpart <a href="/wiki/Jenny_Macklin" title="Jenny Macklin">Jenny Macklin</a>, who said that "Scott Morrison was appointed to clean up <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Andrews_(politician)" title="Kevin Andrews (politician)">Kevin Andrews</a>' mess but left behind more chaos, confusion and cuts."<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2015, three hundred alumni of Sydney Boys High School signed a letter protesting Morrison's attendance at an alumni fund-raising event. The protest letter expressed the opinion that the school should not celebrate a person who has "so flagrantly disregarded human rights."<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During May 2015, Morrison promoted his plan for a $3.5 billion overhaul of the childcare subsidies system. His substantial advertising efforts led to claims that he was overshadowing the role of Treasurer <a href="/wiki/Joe_Hockey" title="Joe Hockey">Joe Hockey</a>. Morrison insisted that he did not desire to take over the position of Treasurer despite his strong performances.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morrison was the minister who established the <a href="/wiki/Robodebt_scheme" title="Robodebt scheme">Robodebt scheme</a> that sent out an estimated 526,000 incorrect automated debt notices to mostly low-income Australians. This scheme was later found by the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_the_Robodebt_Scheme" title="Royal Commission into the Robodebt Scheme">Royal Commission into the Robodebt Scheme</a> to be unlawful and "cruel and crude".<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Royal Commission report stated that "Mr Morrison allowed cabinet to be misled because he did not make that obvious inquiry. He took the proposal to cabinet without necessary information as to what it actually entailed and without the caveat that it required legislative and policy change to permit the use of the ATO PAYG data in the way proposed in circumstances where: he knew that the proposal still involved income averaging; only a few weeks previously he had been told of that caveat; nothing had changed in the proposal; and he had done nothing to ascertain why the caveat no longer applied. He failed to meet his ministerial responsibility to ensure that cabinet was properly informed about what the proposal actually entailed and to ensure that it was lawful."<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Turnbull_government_.282015.E2.80.932018.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Turnbull_government_(2015–2018)">Turnbull government (2015–2018)</span></h3>
<p>Morrison was appointed as Treasurer in the <a href="/wiki/Turnbull_government" title="Turnbull government">Turnbull government</a> in September 2015, replacing <a href="/wiki/Joe_Hockey" title="Joe Hockey">Joe Hockey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> In his first press conference as Treasurer, he indicated a reduction in government expenditure and stated that the Mid-Year Economic and Fiscal Outlook (MYEFO) and <a href="/wiki/White_Paper" class="mw-redirect" title="White Paper">White Paper</a> on tax reform would arrive on time.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 2016, Morrison handed down the <a href="/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_budget" title="2016 Australian federal budget">2016 Australian federal budget</a>. It included the introduction of a 40 percent diverted profits tax (popularly known as the "<a href="/wiki/Google_tax" title="Google tax">Google tax</a>"), which is an <a href="/wiki/Anti-avoidance" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-avoidance">anti-avoidance</a> measure designed to prevent <a href="/wiki/Base_erosion_and_profit_shifting" title="Base erosion and profit shifting">base erosion and profit shifting</a>. It was passed into law as the <i>Diverted Profits Tax Act 2017</i> and took effect on 1 July 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The new tax received criticism from some quarters, with the Corporate Tax Association stating that it would have "unpredictable outcomes" and negatively affect Australian business.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2017, Morrison addressed the House of Representatives while holding a lump of <a href="/wiki/Coal" title="Coal">coal</a>, stating "This is coal. Don't be afraid. Don't be scared. It won't hurt you," and accusing those concerned about the <a href="/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_the_coal_industry" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental impact of the coal industry">environmental impact of the coal industry</a> of having "an ideological, pathological fear of coal."<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> He handed down the <a href="/wiki/2017_Australian_federal_budget" title="2017 Australian federal budget">2017 Australian federal budget</a> in May 2017, reporting a $33.2 billion deficit.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2017, the government introduced the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_Misconduct_in_the_Banking,_Superannuation_and_Financial_Services_Industry" title="Royal Commission into Misconduct in the Banking, Superannuation and Financial Services Industry">Royal Commission into Misconduct in the Banking, Superannuation and Financial Services Industry</a> (popularly known as the Banking Royal Commission). Morrison originally opposed the creation of a <a href="/wiki/Royal_commission" title="Royal commission">royal commission</a>, believing that a Senate inquiry would be sufficient. He voted against a royal commission 23 times between April 2016 and June 2017, and in September 2016 described it as "nothing more than crass populism seeking to undermine confidence in the banking and financial system, which is key to jobs and growth in this country."<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> In announcing that the royal commission would take place, Morrison described it as a "regrettable but necessary action."<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> In response to the commission's findings, in April 2018 he announced the introduction of new criminal and civil penalties for financial misconduct, including potential prison sentences of 10 years for individuals and fines of up to $210 million for companies.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Morrison handed down the <a href="/wiki/2018_Australian_federal_budget" title="2018 Australian federal budget">2018 Australian federal budget</a> on 8 May, reporting a $14.5 billion deficit.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> He subsequently rejected calls to increase the rate of the <a href="/wiki/Social_security_in_Australia" title="Social security in Australia">Newstart Allowance</a>, saying "my priority is to give tax relief to people who are working and paying taxes."<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_of_Australia_.282018.E2.80.932022.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_of_Australia_(2018–2022)">Prime Minister of Australia (2018–2022)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Morrison_government" title="Morrison government">Morrison government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership_election">Leadership election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/2018_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spills" title="2018 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills">2018 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills</a></div>
