<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_(44304308550).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_%2844304308550%29.jpg/220px-President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_%2844304308550%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_%2844304308550%29.jpg/330px-President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_%2844304308550%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_%2844304308550%29.jpg/440px-President_Donald_J._Trump_at_the_G20_Summit_%2844304308550%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Morrison with U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2018_G20_Buenos_Aires_summit" title="2018 G20 Buenos Aires summit">G20 Summit</a> in <a href="/wiki/Buenos_Aires" title="Buenos Aires">Buenos Aires</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:P20210921AS-1186_(51705713647).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/P20210921AS-1186_%2851705713647%29.jpg/220px-P20210921AS-1186_%2851705713647%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/P20210921AS-1186_%2851705713647%29.jpg/330px-P20210921AS-1186_%2851705713647%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/P20210921AS-1186_%2851705713647%29.jpg/440px-P20210921AS-1186_%2851705713647%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4192" data-file-height="2795" /></a><figcaption>Morrison with U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Joe_Biden" title="Joe Biden">Joe Biden</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Seventy-sixth_session_of_the_United_Nations_General_Assembly" title="Seventy-sixth session of the United Nations General Assembly">UN General Assembly</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg/220px-2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg/330px-2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg/440px-2019-08-30_Scott_Morrison_visits_Francisco_Guterres_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1695" data-file-height="1130" /></a><figcaption>Morrison with East Timor's president <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Guterres" title="Francisco Guterres">Francisco Guterres</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a> called a <a href="/wiki/Leadership_spill" title="Leadership spill">leadership spill</a> on 21 August 2018 in order to gauge the confidence of the Liberal Party in his leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> He defeated challenger <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> by 48 votes to 35.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Over the following days, there was repeated speculation about a second spill being called, without Turnbull's approval. Turnbull announced two days later that he would resign the leadership if a spill motion were passed.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Dutton, Morrison and <a href="/wiki/Julie_Bishop" title="Julie Bishop">Julie Bishop</a> announced they would stand for the leadership if that were the case.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A spill motion was passed on 24 August by 45 votes to 40, and Turnbull did not run as a candidate in the resulting leadership vote. On the first ballot, Dutton received 38 votes, Morrison 36 votes, and Bishop 11 votes. On the second ballot, Morrison received 45 votes and Dutton 40 votes. He thus became leader of the Liberal Party and prime minister-designate. <a href="/wiki/Josh_Frydenberg" title="Josh Frydenberg">Josh Frydenberg</a> was elected as the party's deputy leader, in place of Bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Morrison was widely seen as a compromise candidate, who was agreeable to both the moderate supporters of Turnbull and Bishop and conservatives concerned about Dutton's electability.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> He was sworn in as prime minister on the evening of 24 August.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Upon assumption of office, Morrison would become Australia's fifth prime minister in eleven years, and the fourth prime minister in that time to enter office through a leadership spill.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Several months later, Morrison introduced new criteria for leadership spills, requiring that a two-thirds majority vote from party members would be required to trigger one, in an attempt to stop "coup culture".<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Soon after Morrison was sworn in, Nationals <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a> <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Hogan_(politician)" title="Kevin Hogan (politician)">Kevin Hogan</a> moved to the crossbench in protest of the wave of Liberal spills.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Although Hogan continued to support the Coalition on confidence and supply and remained in the National party room, his departure to the crossbench and Turnbull's retirement from politics reduced the Coalition to a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> of 74 seats.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> The Morrison government remained in minority after Turnbull's seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wentworth" title="Division of Wentworth">Wentworth</a> was lost to independent <a href="/wiki/Kerryn_Phelps" title="Kerryn Phelps">Kerryn Phelps</a> at a <a href="/wiki/2018_Wentworth_by-election" title="2018 Wentworth by-election">by-election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="First_term.2C_2018.E2.80.932019"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term,_2018–2019">First term, 2018–2019</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Morrison_ministry" title="First Morrison ministry">First Morrison ministry</a></div>
<p>Morrison made his first overseas trip as prime minister less than a week after acceding to the office. He visited the <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesian</a> capital of <a href="/wiki/Jakarta" title="Jakarta">Jakarta</a> for the Australia–Indonesia Business Forum and met with President <a href="/wiki/Joko_Widodo" title="Joko Widodo">Joko Widodo</a>, announcing the <a href="/wiki/Indonesia%E2%80%93Australia_Comprehensive_Economic_Partnership_Agreement" title="Indonesia–Australia Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement">Indonesia–Australia Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement</a> that had been negotiated under the preceding Turnbull government.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2018, Morrison announced Australia was reviewing whether to move Australia's embassy in Israel from <a href="/wiki/Tel_Aviv" title="Tel Aviv">Tel Aviv</a> to <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>, and recognising Jerusalem as the <a href="/wiki/Status_of_Jerusalem" title="Status of Jerusalem">capital of Israel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> In December 2018,  Morrison announced Australia has recognised <a href="/wiki/West_Jerusalem" title="West Jerusalem">West Jerusalem</a> as the capital of Israel but will not immediately move its embassy from Tel Aviv.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2018, Morrison privately raised the issue of <a href="/wiki/Xinjiang_re-education_camps" class="mw-redirect" title="Xinjiang re-education camps">Xinjiang re-education camps</a> and human rights abuses against the <a href="/wiki/Uyghurs" title="Uyghurs">Uyghur</a> <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_China" title="Islam in China">Muslim</a> minority in a meeting with <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China" title="Premier of the People&#39;s Republic of China">Chinese Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Li_Keqiang" title="Li Keqiang">Li Keqiang</a> in Singapore.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2019, Morrison condemned the <a href="/wiki/Christchurch_mosque_shootings" title="Christchurch mosque shootings">Christchurch mosque shootings</a> as an "extremist, right-wing violent terrorist attack." He also stated that Australians and New Zealanders were family and that the Australian authorities would be cooperating with New Zealand authorities to assist with the investigation.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Morrison condemned "reckless" and "highly offensive" comments made by <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>'s President <a href="/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan" title="Recep Tayyip Erdoğan">Recep Tayyip Erdoğan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>  Erdoğan repeatedly showed video taken by the <a href="/wiki/Christchurch" title="Christchurch">Christchurch</a> mosque shooter to his supporters at campaign rallies for upcoming<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Scott_Morrison&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/2019_Turkish_local_elections" title="2019 Turkish local elections">local elections</a> and said Australians and New Zealanders who came to Turkey with anti-Muslim sentiments "would be sent back in coffins like their grandfathers were" during the <a href="/wiki/Gallipoli_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Gallipoli Campaign">Gallipoli Campaign</a> of World War I.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2019_federal_election">2019 federal election</span></h3>
<p>Morrison led the Coalition into the <a href="/wiki/2019_Australian_federal_election" title="2019 Australian federal election">2019 election</a>. At time of the writs being issued, the Coalition had been behind the Labor Party in most opinion polls for previous term of parliament, leading to widespread expectations that the Coalition would lose.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPatrick2019118_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatrick2019118-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> However, in a significant upset, the Coalition retained its majority.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> This was considered to have been caused by the unpopularity of opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Bill_Shorten" title="Bill Shorten">Bill Shorten</a> and Labor's failure to adapt to the re-framing of the election as a choice between Morrison and Shorten.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKelly202124_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly202124-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Claiming victory on election night, Morrison stated that he had "always believed in miracles".<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Second_term.2C_2019.E2.80.932022"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term,_2019–2022">Second term, 2019–2022</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Second_Morrison_ministry" title="Second Morrison ministry">Second Morrison ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:-G7Biarritz_(48622478973).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/-G7Biarritz_%2848622478973%29.jpg/220px--G7Biarritz_%2848622478973%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/-G7Biarritz_%2848622478973%29.jpg/330px--G7Biarritz_%2848622478973%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/-G7Biarritz_%2848622478973%29.jpg/440px--G7Biarritz_%2848622478973%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1333" /></a><figcaption>Morrison at <a href="/wiki/45th_G7_summit" title="45th G7 summit">45th G7 summit</a> in <a href="/wiki/Biarritz" title="Biarritz">Biarritz</a>, France</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_affairs">Domestic affairs</span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Bushfires">Bushfires</span></h5>
<p>In December 2019, Morrison faced criticism for taking an unannounced overseas holiday with his family to <a href="/wiki/Hawaii" title="Hawaii">Hawaii</a>, United States during the <a href="/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Australian_bushfire_season" title="2019–20 Australian bushfire season">2019–20 Australian bushfire season</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-abc_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Morrison's office initially declined to comment on the length of his trip and his whereabouts, citing security concerns, and made false claims that Morrison was not in Hawaii.<sup id="cite_ref-abc_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> After increasing criticism from opposition politicians and on social media regarding the holiday, Morrison released a statement on 20 December that stated he "deeply regret[ted] any offence caused" and that he would cut his holiday short to return to Australia on 21 December.<sup id="cite_ref-abc_130-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-sbs_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sbs-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 22 November 2021, Leader of the Opposition <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Albanese" title="Anthony Albanese">Anthony Albanese</a> accused Morrison of falsely claiming in Parliament that he had informed him that he was travelling to Hawaii.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Response_to_parliamentary_sexual_misconduct_allegations">Response to parliamentary sexual misconduct allegations</span></h5>
<p>The Morrison government was widely criticised for its handling of the <a href="/wiki/2021_Australian_Parliament_House_sexual_misconduct_allegations" title="2021 Australian Parliament House sexual misconduct allegations">2021 Australian Parliament House sexual misconduct allegations</a>, with an <a href="/wiki/Essential_poll" class="mw-redirect" title="Essential poll">Essential poll</a> finding that 65% of respondents (including 76% of Labor supporters, 51% of Coalition supporters and 88% of Greens supporters) saying the government was more interested in protecting itself than women.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> As well as the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> and <a href="/wiki/Australian_Greens" title="Australian Greens">Greens</a> parties, the government faced criticism from within its own party. Former prime minister Malcolm Turnbull, who had been made aware of the allegations against <a href="/wiki/Christian_Porter" title="Christian Porter">Christian Porter</a> in 2019, criticised him for taking too long to come forward.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Former prime minister <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> defended Morrison's decision not to open an independent inquiry into Porter's conduct.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Australian_of_the_Year" title="Australian of the Year">Australian of the Year</a> and sexual assault survivor advocate <a href="/wiki/Grace_Tame" title="Grace Tame">Grace Tame</a> also criticised Morrison in a speech to the National Press Club, saying she did not believe he was creating an environment where victims were believed. She also said "It shouldn't take having children to have a conscience" in response to Morrison's statement that he'd been prompted to reflect on the issue and decide to listen to Tame after a discussion with his wife <a href="/wiki/Jenny_Morrison" title="Jenny Morrison">Jenny Morrison</a> where she said to him "you have to think about this as a father. What would you want to happen if it were our girls?"<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Porter resigned from his parliamentary position in September following concerns that he had accepted anonymous donations via a <a href="/wiki/Blind_trust" title="Blind trust">blind trust</a> to pay for his legal expenses.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> The following month, Morrison and the Coalition successfully voted against a motion to investigate Porter's blind trust, in turn preventing the identities of the benefactors who donated to his legal costs from being revealed.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the Australian House of Representatives">Speaker of the House of Representatives</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tony_Smith_(Victorian_politician)" title="Tony Smith (Victorian politician)">Tony Smith</a>, had determined that there was a <a href="/wiki/Prima_facie" title="Prima facie">prima facie</a> case and, in voting down the motion, the Morrison government became the first government since <a href="/wiki/Federation_of_Australia" title="Federation of Australia">Federation</a> to refuse a referral from the Speaker.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> This decision attracted significant attention and criticism from the media.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Afghan_refugees">Afghan refugees</span></h5>
<p>Morrison was also criticised for not accepting more <a href="/wiki/Afghan_refugees" title="Afghan refugees">Afghan refugees</a>, who were fleeing the country after the <a href="/wiki/Taliban" title="Taliban">Taliban</a> <a href="/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive" title="2021 Taliban offensive">seized control of the country</a> in August 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> He agreed to give <a href="/wiki/Humanitarian_visa" title="Humanitarian visa">humanitarian visas</a> to 3,000 Afghan refugees, fewer than other countries such as the United States and the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Morrison later determined that more than 3,000 refugees may be accepted, as the original number was a "floor not a ceiling".<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> He would later state that he would only resettle refugees who came in through "official channels", and those who came to Australia via boat would not receive permanent residency.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Eastern_Australia_floods">Eastern Australia floods</span></h5>
<p>Morrison was heavily condemned for his government's response to the <a href="/wiki/2022_eastern_Australia_floods" title="2022 eastern Australia floods">2022 eastern Australia floods</a>; criticism was levelled against him for campaigning in <a href="/wiki/Perth" title="Perth">Perth</a> instead of being present in New South Wales, causing a relief package for flood victims to be delayed, with many critics suggesting that Morrison was prioritising marketing over the flood response.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> That same month, Morrison was named Australia's least trusted politician in a study by <a href="/wiki/Roy_Morgan_Research" class="mw-redirect" title="Roy Morgan Research">Roy Morgan Research</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Joint_ministerial_positions">Joint ministerial positions</span></h5>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison_ministerial_positions_controversy" title="Scott Morrison ministerial positions controversy">Scott Morrison ministerial positions controversy</a></div>
<p>On 15 August 2022, <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">Prime Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Albanese" title="Anthony Albanese">Anthony Albanese</a> announced an investigation by his office into claims that Morrison secretly appointed himself to administer several government departments during the COVID-19 pandemic.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The health minister at the time, <a href="/wiki/Greg_Hunt" title="Greg Hunt">Greg Hunt</a>, was understood to have agreed to Morrison's position administering the health department;  however, the finance minister, <a href="/wiki/Mathias_Cormann" title="Mathias Cormann">Mathias Cormann</a>, was unaware that Morrison had appointed himself in a joint ministerial position. The resources minister, <a href="/wiki/Keith_Pitt" title="Keith Pitt">Keith Pitt</a>, was aware of Morrison's self-appointment to the resources portfolio "sometime in 2021".<sup id="cite_ref-:2_157-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/David_Littleproud" title="David Littleproud">David Littleproud</a>, who was the agriculture minister in Morrison's government, criticised the self-appointments as "pretty ordinary<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup>".<sup id="cite_ref-:2_157-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later that day, the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a>'s office confirmed that Morrison had been appointed to a number of ministerial offices, without stating which. As a change in the responsibilities of an existing minister, this had not required further swearing in but had been done by an administrative instrument; announcement of the appointments had been a matter for the government.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Constitutional law professor <a href="/wiki/Anne_Twomey_(academic)" title="Anne Twomey (academic)">Anne Twomey</a>, however, could not trace such instruments and called the process "just bizarre".<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>  Prime Minister Anthony Albanese ordered a search for them and is seeking legal advice from the solicitor-general.  Leading members of the Morrison cabinet, not only Mathias Cormann but also <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> (then defence minister and now leader of the opposition), have said that they had not been aware of these appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>  Morrison initially declined to comment but later phoned <a href="/wiki/2GB" title="2GB">2GB</a> to say that these three appointments—there might have been more, but he was unsure—had been "extraordinary measures" as "safeguards" during the Covid pandemic.  He had got himself appointed as joint resources minister in order to be able to overrule a decision on gas exploration by the resources minister.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following day, 16 August, Albanese held a second press conference, confirming that Morrison was appointed to administer five departments in addition to his role administering the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_the_Prime_Minister_and_Cabinet_(Australia)" title="Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet (Australia)">Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:3_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-169">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Morrison was appointed to administer the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Health_and_Aged_Care" title="Department of Health and Aged Care">Department of Health</a> on 14 March 2020; the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Finance_(Australia)" title="Department of Finance (Australia)">Department of Finance</a> on 30 March 2020; the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Home_Affairs_(Australia)" title="Department of Home Affairs (Australia)">Department of Home Affairs</a> on 6 May 2021, the <a href="/wiki/Treasury_(Australia)" title="Treasury (Australia)">Department of the Treasury</a> on 6 May 2021, and the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Industry,_Science,_Energy_and_Resources" title="Department of Industry, Science, Energy and Resources">Department of Industry, Science, Energy and Resources</a> on 15 April 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-169">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Documents relating to these appointments were released by the Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet on 17 August.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Albanese asked the <a href="/wiki/Solicitor-General_of_Australia" title="Solicitor-General of Australia">Solicitor-General</a> for an opinion on the validity of the appointment to the resources ministry.  The report, released on 23 August, advised that this appointment had been lawful, but that its secrecy "fundamentally undermined" the principles of responsible government.<sup id="cite_ref-Channel_9_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Channel_9-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Anthony Albanese announced an inquiry into Scott Morrison's ministerial positions, to be headed by former <a href="/wiki/List_of_Justices_of_the_High_Court_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Justices of the High Court of Australia">High Court Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Bell_(judge)" title="Virginia Bell (judge)">Virginia Bell</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> 
On 25 November 2022, Bell reported that Morrison's appointment to multiple ministerial positions was "corrosive" to trust in government. She recommended legislation to ensure all ministerial appointments were made public.<sup id="cite_ref-Evans_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evans-174">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 30 November 2022, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> voted 86–50 for a government motion to censure Scott Morrison for failing to disclose to the parliament and the public his secret appointments to a number of ministries. All non-Coalition votes were in favour, as well as that of Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Bridget_Archer" title="Bridget Archer">Bridget Archer</a>;  all Coalition votes were against, as well as that of <a href="/wiki/Bob_Katter" title="Bob Katter">Bob Katter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Karp_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Karp-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Tribunal_appointments">Tribunal appointments</span></h5>
<p>Two former senior judges have reported to the <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia" title="Attorney-General of Australia">Commonwealth Attorney-General</a> that, shortly before the <a href="/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election" title="2022 Australian federal election">2022 Australian federal election</a> was called, the Morrison government got an unusual number of people with political connections to the Coalition appointed to highly paid positions on the <a href="/wiki/Administrative_Appeals_Tribunal" title="Administrative Appeals Tribunal">Administrative Appeals Tribunal</a>. Each was appointed by the Governor-General through an administrative instrument, but the appointment was not gazetted, although there was at least one media release. In some cases the appointee was already a member of the AAT whose term still had some time to run. In no case did the new appointment carry a specified term of office. The two former judges question whether these appointments were validly made and whether, if they were not valid, that would affect the validity of decisions that these appointees have made.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Australian_Future_Leaders_Foundation">Australian Future Leaders Foundation</span></h5>
<p>The Australian Future Leaders Foundation, a leadership education programme proposed by <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> David Hurley, was formally established in 2021.  The Morrison government allocated it $18m in funding over five years, with no formal process of approval.  The Senate Standing Committee for the Scrutiny of Delegated Legislation was seeking further information and no public money had yet been paid,<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> although Hurley had hosted fundraising events at <a href="/wiki/Government_House,_Canberra" title="Government House, Canberra">Government House</a>, when the Albanese government cancelled the grant.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg/220px-Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="125" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg/330px-Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg/440px-Prime_Minister_Scott_Morrison_delivering_the_Lowy_Lecture_on_2_October_2019_at_Sydney_Town_Hall.jpg 2x" data-file-width="868" data-file-height="492" /></a><figcaption>At the 2019 Lowy Lecture, Scott Morrison argued that the "distinctiveness of independent nations is preserved within a framework of mutual respect".</figcaption></figure>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="2019_Hong_Kong_protests">2019 Hong Kong protests</span></h5>
<p>In August 2019, Morrison called on the <a href="/wiki/Carrie_Lam_as_Chief_Executive_of_Hong_Kong" class="mw-redirect" title="Carrie Lam as Chief Executive of Hong Kong">Chief Executive of Hong Kong</a> to listen to protester demands, denying that the <a href="/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Hong_Kong_protests" class="mw-redirect" title="2019–20 Hong Kong protests">2019–20 Hong Kong protests</a> were showing signs of terrorism.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="2019_Turkish_offensive">2019 Turkish offensive</span></h5>
<p>In October 2019, Morrison criticised the <a href="/wiki/2019_Turkish_offensive_into_north-eastern_Syria" title="2019 Turkish offensive into north-eastern Syria">2019 Turkish offensive into north-eastern Syria</a>. Morrison stated that he was concerned for the safety of the <a href="/wiki/Kurds_in_Syria" title="Kurds in Syria">Kurds</a> living in the region and also feared that the offensive could result in a resurgence of <a href="/wiki/ISIS" class="mw-redirect" title="ISIS">ISIS</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span id="Sino.E2.80.93Australian_relations"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Sino–Australian_relations">Sino–Australian relations</span></h5>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93China_relations#Morrison_government" title="Australia–China relations">Australia–China relations §&#160;Morrison government</a></div>
<p>On 30 November 2020, a Chinese diplomat, <a href="/wiki/Zhao_Lijian" title="Zhao Lijian">Zhao Lijian</a>, posted on his Twitter page a digitally manipulated image of an Australian soldier who appeared to hold a bloodied knife against the throat of an <a href="/wiki/Afghanistan" title="Afghanistan">Afghan</a> child.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-abc-cg_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc-cg-182">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> The image is believed to be a reference to the <a href="/wiki/Brereton_Report" title="Brereton Report">Brereton Report</a>, which had been released earlier by the Australian government that month, and which details <a href="/wiki/Military_history_of_Australia_during_the_War_in_Afghanistan#War_crimes_inquiry" title="Military history of Australia during the War in Afghanistan">war crimes</a> committed by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Defence_Force" title="Australian Defence Force">Australian Defence Force</a> during the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%93present)" class="mw-redirect" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–present)">War in Afghanistan</a> between 2005 and 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Later that day, Morrison called a press conference, calling the image "offensive" and "truly repugnant",<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> and demanding a formal apology from the Chinese government. China rejected the demands for an apology on the following day,<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> with the artist of the image creating another artwork <i>To Morrison</i> in response to Morrison's demand.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> The incident had the effect of unifying Australian politicians in condemning China across party lines while also drawing attention to the Brereton Report.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> The incident was further seen as a sign of deteriorating <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93China_relations" title="Australia–China relations">relations between Australia and China</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_(2).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_%282%29.jpg/220px-Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_%282%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_%282%29.jpg/330px-Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_%282%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_%282%29.jpg/440px-Fumio_Kishida_met_with_Australian_PM_Morrison_at_the_COP26_%282%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1380" /></a><figcaption>Morrison and Japanese Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Fumio_Kishida" title="Fumio Kishida">Fumio Kishida</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference" title="2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference">COP26 climate summit</a> in Glasgow on 2 November 2021</figcaption></figure>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Relations_with_New_Zealand">Relations with New Zealand</span></h5>
<p>As Prime Minister, Morrison has defended Australia's policy of deporting non-citizens including New Zealanders who had violated its character test or committed crimes. This policy was criticised by his <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_New_Zealand" title="Prime Minister of New Zealand">New Zealand counterpart</a> <a href="/wiki/Jacinda_Ardern" title="Jacinda Ardern">Jacinda Ardern</a>, who described it as "corrosive" to <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations" title="Australia–New Zealand relations">Australia–New Zealand relations</a> in February 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In mid-February 2021, Morrison defended the Australian policy of revoking Australian citizenship for dual nationals engaged in terrorism. The previous year, the Australian government had revoked the citizenship of dual Australian–New Zealand citizen <a href="/wiki/Suhayra_Aden" title="Suhayra Aden">Suhayra Aden</a>, who had become an <a href="/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant">ISIS</a>  <a href="/wiki/Brides_of_ISIL" class="mw-redirect" title="Brides of ISIL">bride</a>. New Zealand Prime Minister Ardern had criticised the decision, accusing Australia of abandoning its citizens.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC_News_16_Feb_2020_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC_News_16_Feb_2020-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Al_Jazeera_16_Feb_21_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Al_Jazeera_16_Feb_21-192">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> Following a phone conversation, the two leaders agreed to work together in the "spirit of the Australian-New Zealand relationship" to address what Ardern described as "quite a complex legal situation."<sup id="cite_ref-Stuff_phone_conversation_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuff_phone_conversation-193">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late May 2021, Morrison made his first state visit to New Zealand since the COVID-19 <a href="/wiki/Lockdown" title="Lockdown">lockdown</a>, meeting New Zealand Prime Minister Ardern in <a href="/wiki/Queenstown,_New_Zealand" title="Queenstown, New Zealand">Queenstown</a>. The two heads of governments issued a joint statement affirming bilateral cooperation on the issues of COVID-19, bilateral relations, and security issues in the Indo-Pacific. Morrison and Ardern also raised concerns about the <a href="/wiki/South_China_Sea" title="South China Sea">South China Sea</a> dispute and human rights in Hong Kong and Xinjiang.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-AFR_NZ_visit_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AFR_NZ_visit-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> In response to the joint statement, Chinese <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_(China)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ministry of Foreign Affairs (China)">Foreign Ministry</a> spokesperson Wang Wenbin criticised the Australian and New Zealand governments for interfering in Chinese domestic affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-AFR_NZ_visit_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AFR_NZ_visit-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> During the visit, Morrison defended Australia's decision to revoke ISIS bride Suhayra Aden's citizenship but indicated that the Australian Government would consider allowing her children to settle in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine">2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine</span></h5>
<p>In February 2022, Morrison condemned Russian President <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Putin" title="Vladimir Putin">Vladimir Putin</a> for launching the <a href="/wiki/2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine" class="mw-redirect" title="2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine">2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine</a> and imposed sanctions on travel bans on individuals perceived to be supporting the invasion.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> Morrison said Australia would begin sending lethal aid to the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Ukraine" title="Government of Ukraine">Ukrainian government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> The Australian government moved to join with the United Kingdom, Canada, and the United States in personally sanctioning Putin and <a href="/wiki/Sergey_Lavrov" title="Sergey Lavrov">Sergey Lavrov</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Russia)" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs (Russia)">foreign minister</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="AUKUS">AUKUS</span></h5>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/AUKUS" title="AUKUS">AUKUS</a></div>
<p>In September 2021, Morrison, British premier <a href="/wiki/Boris_Johnson" title="Boris Johnson">Boris Johnson</a> and US President <a href="/wiki/Joe_Biden" title="Joe Biden">Joe Biden</a> announced <a href="/wiki/AUKUS" title="AUKUS">AUKUS</a>, a security pact between Australia, the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> seen as an initiative to counter the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_China" title="Foreign policy of China">perceived dominance of China in the Pacific</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> This superseded a proposed submarine pact between Australia and <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> that had been in discussions at the same time; the announcement of AUKUS attracted backlash from French officials and damaged <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93France_relations" title="Australia–France relations">Australia–France relations</a>. Chinese officials also criticised the agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/President_of_France" title="President of France">French President</a> <a href="/wiki/Emmanuel_Macron" title="Emmanuel Macron">Emmanuel Macron</a> reportedly remarked privately to Morrison that the dissolution of the agreement had "broke the relationship of trust" between the two countries,<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> and publicly accused him of lying during the <a href="/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference" title="2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference">2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference</a>, which was seen as damaging to Morrison's public image.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="COVID-19_pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Australia">COVID-19 pandemic in Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_Australia" title="COVID-19 vaccination in Australia">COVID-19 vaccination in Australia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg/220px-National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="123" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg/330px-National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg/440px-National_Cabinet_meeting.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1576" data-file-height="880" /></a><figcaption>Morrison at a National Cabinet meeting</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Australia">COVID-19 pandemic in Australia</a> prompted Morrison to establish the <a href="/wiki/National_Cabinet_(Australia)" title="National Cabinet (Australia)">National Cabinet</a> on 13 March 2020. This body is composed of the prime minister and the <a href="/wiki/Premiers_and_chief_ministers_of_the_Australian_states_and_territories" title="Premiers and chief ministers of the Australian states and territories">premiers and chief ministers of the states and territories</a> to coordinate the national response to the pandemic.<sup id="cite_ref-natcab1_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-natcab1-212">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> On 29 May 2020, the Prime Minister announced that the National Cabinet would replace the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Australian_Governments" title="Council of Australian Governments">Council of Australian Governments</a> (COAG) and meetings after the pandemic would be held monthly, instead of the <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/biannual" class="extiw" title="wikt:biannual">biannual</a> meetings of COAG.<sup id="cite_ref-COAG_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-COAG-213">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1062260506">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:50%; ;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>"The world over - we have all faced the health and economic crises generated by the COVID-19 pandemic. 2020 is a year none of us want to repeat. In Australia, we have used our strong balance sheet - built up over many years of discipline, to support and provide our health system with the additional resources, record levels, it has needed - and to provide major, unprecedented economic supports for households and businesses - providing much needed strength and resilience to the economy to both cushion the blow and to recover...... As the world's only nation continent, we always have to be outward looking. You don't get rich by selling stuff to yourself. Singaporeans certainly understand that."
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><cite class="left-aligned" style="">Morrison speaking about the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic on Australia at the <a href="/wiki/Singapore_FinTech_Festival" title="Singapore FinTech Festival">Singapore FinTech Festival</a>, December 2020<sup id="cite_ref-PMA_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PMA-214">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup></cite>
</p>
</div>
<p>On 5 May, Morrison, New Zealand prime minister Jacinda Ardern and Australian state and territorial leaders agreed to work together to develop a <a href="/wiki/Trans-Tasman" title="Trans-Tasman">Trans-Tasman</a> travel zone that would allow residents from both countries to travel freely between them without restrictions.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Morrison supported an international inquiry into the origins of the global <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</a> and opined that the coronavirus most likely originated in a wildlife wet market in <a href="/wiki/Wuhan" title="Wuhan">Wuhan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 2 March 2022 Morrison announced he had contracted COVID and was suffering from flu-like symptoms.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Vaccine_rollout">Vaccine rollout</span></h5>
<p>In August 2020, Morrison announced that Australians would be "among the first in the world to receive a COVID-19 vaccine, if it proves successful, through an agreement between the Australian government and UK-based drug company <a href="/wiki/AstraZeneca" title="AstraZeneca">AstraZeneca</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> In November 2020, he said the government's COVID-19 strategy would put "Australia at the front of the queue for a safe and effective vaccine".<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During a press conference on 7 January 2021, Morrison announced that Australia's vaccination program would begin in February of that year, also stating that the government planned to vaccinate four million people by the end of March.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> However, this figure was not met, as only 600,000 doses were administered by 31 March, 3.4 million less than the target.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Both the original goal for vaccine doses and vaccine priority cohorts were revised several times. By 30 June 2021 the number of doses given (7.6 million) was 4.7 million less than the goal for the end of June.<sup id="cite_ref-Nicholas_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nicholas-223">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The slow vaccine rollout prompted traditionally conservative newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a></i> to editorialise that "the federal government is losing credibility with its management of the vaccine rollout and its repeated claims that everything is on track".<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Former ALP staffer <a href="/wiki/Tim_Soutphommasane" title="Tim Soutphommasane">Tim Soutphommasane</a> and progressive activist <a href="/wiki/Marc_Stears" title="Marc Stears">Marc Stears</a> criticised the government's management of the vaccine rollout in June 2021, saying it will likely be "taught as a case study of public policy failure".<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> During a press conference in July, Morrison issued an apology for the slow vaccine rollout.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> In August, Morrison declared that the government's problems with the rollout had been "overcome", despite several states having a shortage of vaccines.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> To describe Australia's prolonged vaccination rollout, trade unionist <a href="/wiki/Sally_McManus" title="Sally McManus">Sally McManus</a> coined the term "strollout", with the phrase being named as the country's <a href="/wiki/Word_of_the_year" title="Word of the year">word of the year</a> by <a href="/wiki/Macquarie_Dictionary" title="Macquarie Dictionary">Macquarie Dictionary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Internal_criticism">Internal criticism</span></h3>
<p>Morrison was subject to internal criticism from colleagues and former colleagues in 2021 and 2022.
</p><p>In November 2021, Morrison's handling of a proposed submarine pact with France led his predecessor Malcolm Turnbull to comment on Morrison's credibility.  ABC News quoted Turnbull as saying, "[Morrison] can twist and turn and leak a text message here and leak a document there to his stenographic friends in the media, but ultimately the failure here was one of not being honest."<sup id="cite_ref-:20_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:20-232">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January and February 2022, texts from his Coalition colleagues were leaked. In January 2022, texts between former New South Wales premier <a href="/wiki/Gladys_Berejiklian" title="Gladys Berejiklian">Gladys Berejiklian</a> and an unnamed senior cabinet minister in the Morrison government were revealed, wherein Morrison was labelled a "horrible, horrible person" by the former premier and a "complete psycho" by the minister.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> This was soon followed by leaked texts sent from deputy prime minister <a href="/wiki/Barnaby_Joyce" title="Barnaby Joyce">Barnaby Joyce</a>, who in March 2021 accused Morrison of being "a hypocrite and a liar" in text messages.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> Joyce apologised and offered his resignation to Morrison, but it was declined.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March and April 2022, New South Wales state Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Cusack_(politician)" title="Catherine Cusack (politician)">Catherine Cusack</a> accused Morrison of being a "self-serving bully", and using the Eastern Australia floods as a political tactic.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> In April 2022, sitting Liberal Senator <a href="/wiki/Concetta_Fierravanti-Wells" title="Concetta Fierravanti-Wells">Concetta Fierravanti-Wells</a> condemned Morrison as being "unfit for office", as well as an "autocrat", and a "bully" with "no moral compass". She also suggested Morrison had used his religion as a "marketing advantage".<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2022_federal_election">2022 federal election</span></h3>
<p>Morrison sought a second full term at the <a href="/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election" title="2022 Australian federal election">2022 Australian federal election</a>. His primary opposition was the Labor Party, led by <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Albanese" title="Anthony Albanese">Anthony Albanese</a>. The Coalition suffered heavy losses, and it soon became apparent that there was no realistic scenario for Morrison to stay in office. Hours after the polls closed, he conceded defeat to Albanese.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> The Coalition's loss was attributed to Morrison's unpopularity with voters, the popularity of centrist "<a href="/wiki/Teal_independents" title="Teal independents">teal independents</a>" in certain inner-city electorates, and a large swing toward Labor in Western Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> After conceding defeat, Morrison announced that he would step down as leader of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> Soon afterward, he advised the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a>, <a href="/wiki/David_Hurley" title="David Hurley">David Hurley</a>, that he was no longer in a position to govern.
</p><p>Normal practice in Australia calls for a defeated Prime Minister to stay in office as a caretaker until the final results are known. However, the timetable was altered due to the <a href="/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue" title="Quadrilateral Security Dialogue">Quadrilateral Security Dialogue</a> due to begin on 24 May, two days after the election. On 23 May, after securing enough <a href="/wiki/Confidence_and_supply" title="Confidence and supply">confidence and supply</a> support from the crossbench to govern in the event Labor fell short of a majority, Albanese advised Hurley that he could form a government, clearing the way for Morrison to transfer power to Albanese later that day.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A post-election study conducted by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a> in December 2022 revealed that Morrison was the most unpopular major party leader since the beginning of the survey in 1987.<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Asylum_seeker_boat_controversy">Asylum seeker boat controversy</span></h4>
<p>On election day, 21 May 2022, a <a href="/wiki/Suspected_Illegal_Entry_Vessel" class="mw-redirect" title="Suspected Illegal Entry Vessel">Suspected Illegal Entry Vessel</a> was intercepted as a part of Joint Agency Task Force (JATF), <a href="/wiki/Operation_Sovereign_Borders" title="Operation Sovereign Borders">Operation Sovereign Borders</a>. The Prime Minister’s Office then asked the Commander of the JATF to publish a statement about the interception. The incoming Labor government ordered a departmental inquiry, which found that officials had been pressured by the immigration minister, apparently urged by Morrison, to make a quick announcement with release to selected journalists as well as social media, but had refused do so;  although, at the minister's insistence, basic factual information was given limited release in time for a press conference by the prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-249" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-249">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-prime_ministerial_career">Post-prime ministerial career</span></h2>
<p>Soon after the 2022 election, on 30 May, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> was elected as the new leader of the Liberal Party in a <a href="/wiki/2022_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="2022 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">leadership election</a>, with Dutton later stating that Morrison would not be included in his <a href="/wiki/Shadow_ministry_of_Peter_Dutton" title="Shadow ministry of Peter Dutton">shadow ministry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> Morrison remains in Parliament as a backbencher. In May 2023, he took an advisory position with the U.S. based <a href="/wiki/Think_tank" title="Think tank">think tank</a> the <a href="/wiki/Center_for_a_New_American_Security" title="Center for a New American Security">Center for a New American Security</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2022, Morrison gave evidence to the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_the_Robodebt_Scheme" title="Royal Commission into the Robodebt Scheme">Royal Commission into the Robodebt Scheme</a>, concerning his role in the implementation of the unlawful scheme, which was piloted during his time as Social Services minister.<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> The commission handed down its final report in July 2023, with Morrison being criticised for misleading Cabinet and failing his ministerial duties. The commission also found that Morrison gave <a href="/wiki/Perjury" title="Perjury">false evidence</a> to the commission.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Secret_ministerial_appointments_controversy">Secret ministerial appointments controversy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison_ministerial_positions_controversy" title="Scott Morrison ministerial positions controversy">Scott Morrison ministerial positions controversy</a></div>
<p>In August 2022, Morrison was involved in a political scandal when reports emerged that he had secretly advised Governor-General Hurley to appoint him as joint minister for Health, Finance, and Resources between March 2020 and May 2021. In a press conference on 15 August, Albanese announced an investigation into the reports, saying that the people were "kept in the dark" about these developments in a manner that was "unacceptable" and "very contrary to our Westminster system".<sup id="cite_ref-Karp_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Karp-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The following day, Albanese revealed that Morrison had himself appointed as joint Treasurer and joint minister for Home Affairs in addition to the other three portfolios.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>An opinion from the <a href="/wiki/Solicitor-General_of_Australia" title="Solicitor-General of Australia">Solicitor-General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Donaghue" title="Stephen Donaghue">Stephen Donaghue</a>, found that while the appointments may have been lawful and that Hurley was bound by convention to make them, their secrecy "fundamentally undermined" the principles of responsible government. Morrison maintained that "the serious crises facing Australia" demanded that he take on the joint ministries as a "safeguard". He argued that<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="what did he argue? (May 2023)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>.<sup id="cite_ref-Channel_9_171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Channel_9-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> An inquiry by former High Court Justice <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Bell_(judge)" title="Virginia Bell (judge)">Virginia Bell</a> concluded the appointments were "corrosive" to trust in government, and had little connection to pandemic issues. Bell recommended legislation requiring that all ministerial appointments be made public.<sup id="cite_ref-Evans_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evans-174">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 28 November, the House voted to <a href="/wiki/Censure" title="Censure">censure</a> Morrison for the appointments, the first time a former prime minister had been censured. The vote was mostly along party lines, with <a href="/wiki/Bridget_Archer" title="Bridget Archer">Bridget Archer</a> being the only Coalition MP to vote in favour of the motion.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_views">Political views</span></h2>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:transparent;border-top:1px solid #aaa;text-align:center;">Parties</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist"><b>Active</b>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:transparent;border-top:1px solid #aaa;text-align:center;">Related topics</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">
